**Short answer**

- **`type`** – a *keyword* used to create **types/aliases**. It can be assigned, passed around, and is the most flexible way to declare any value (primitives or complex objects).  
- **`interface`** – a special kind of type that defines an **object shape contract**: properties, methods, members, etc. Interfaces are not values themselves; they describe what classes/interfaces must implement.

---

### More detail

| Aspect | `type` | `interface` |
|--------|--------|-------------|
| **Purpose** | Declare a *type* (or alias) that can be used wherever a type is expected. Can also annotate function parameters, return types, variable annotations. | Define the structure of an object or class: properties, methods, members. Serves as a contract for implementation. |
| **Syntax** | `type Name = { … }` (or primitive values like `string`, `number`). | `interface Name { prop1: Type; method(): void; }` |
| **Assignability** | You can assign one type to another (`let x: A = 5; let y: B = x;`) as long as they are compatible. | Interfaces cannot be assigned to variables of a different interface unless the target is `any`. They define *shapes* that classes must satisfy. |
| **Extensibility** | You can extend or redefine types (`type A = B & C`). | You can implement an interface by creating a class (or another interface) and adding members; you cannot “extend” an existing interface directly. |
| **When to use** | • Simple values, function signatures, generic constraints.<br>• When you want the most flexible type system. | • Describing object/class contracts, especially when multiple related classes share properties/methods.<br>• For structural typing (e.g., `implements` in interfaces). |
| **Can be used as a value?** | Yes – it’s an actual type that can hold data. | No – it’s a *declaration* of shape; you implement it via a class or another interface. |

### Example

```ts
// Using 'type' for simple values and function signatures
let name: string = "Alice";          // primitive annotation (also works as alias)
function greet(name: string): void { console.log(`Hello, ${name}`); }

// Using an 'interface' to define a shape of objects/classes
interface Person {
  id: number;
  name: string;
  age?: number;   // optional property
}

class Employee implements Person {
  constructor(public readonly id: number, public name: string) {}
}
```

- `name` is just an annotation (or alias via `type`).
- `Person` tells any class that extends it must have the fields shown.

### Bottom line

- **`type`** = a flexible way to declare *any* type.
- **`interface`** = a specialized, structural contract for object/class shapes. They serve different roles in TypeScript’s type system and are often used together.

// 1. interface CAN be extended directly
interface A { a: string }
interface B extends A { b: number }
const b: B = { a: "x", b: 1 };

// 2. interface CAN be assigned to another interface (structural typing)
interface C { a: string }
const c: C = b; // ok

// 3. interface CAN be used as a type annotation for a variable
const d: A = { a: "y" };

// 4. type CANNOT be used as a value
type T = { x: number };
// const e = T; // would error: 'T' only refers to a type

// 5. declaration merging: interface merges, type does not
interface M { p: string }
interface M { q: number }
const m: M = { p: "a", q: 1 };

// 6. type alias CAN express unions / tuples / primitives / mapped types
type U = "a" | "b";
type Tup = [number, string];
type Prim = string;
type Mapped<T> = { [K in keyof T]: T[K] };

// 7. interface CANNOT express a union
// interface Bad extends U {} // error

// 8. both can be implemented by a class
type Shape = { area(): number };
interface IShape { area(): number }
class Sq implements Shape { area() { return 1 } }
class Ci implements IShape { area() { return 2 } }

// 9. interface can extend a type alias (object type) and vice versa
type ObjT = { z: number };
interface ExtT extends ObjT { w: number }
type Inter = A & { extra: boolean };

// 10. index signature: type alias is more permissive with implicit index
type Dict = { [k: string]: number };
interface IDict { [k: string]: number }

console.log(b, c, d, m, new Sq().area(), new Ci().area());
EOF

`git rebase -i HEAD~3` opens an interactive editor for the last **three commits** before `HEAD` and lets you rewrite or reorder them in "rebase" mode:

- Each selected commit is listed with its current type (e.g., `squash`, `rewrite`, `edit`).
- You can change the type, delete a line entirely, or add new lines to define how that commit will be rewritten relative to the base.
- After saving and committing the changes in this file, Git automatically re-runs those three commits on top of their updated history.

**In plain terms:** it gives you fine-grained control over the last 3 commits — you can squash them into one, reorder them, or keep them as-is while still moving your current branch forward from that point. It's a way to clean up messy commit history before continuing work.

For example, if those three commits were feature branches merged in reverse order and now look wrong, you could mark the first (oldest) one as `squash` or change its base so it lands where you want it.

  ◆ thinking…  ◆ Context: █░░░░░░░░░░┊░░┊░░┊░░ 3% (3.5k/128k) · compact @ 55% (70k) · 1 turns

`git rebase -i HEAD~3` opens an interactive rebase for the **last 3 commits** (the three commits after `HEAD~3`, i.e. `HEAD~3..HEAD`).

**What you see:** your editor opens a todo list, oldest commit first, each line prefixed with a command:

```
pick a1b2c3d Add login form
pick e4f5g6h Fix typo in login form
pick i7j8k9l Add login tests
```

**The commands you can use** (this is the full set — the previous answer got these wrong):

| Command | Short | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| `pick` | `p` | keep the commit as-is |
| `reword` | `r` | keep the changes, edit the commit message |
| `edit` | `e` | stop the rebase at that commit so you can amend it (`git commit --amend`, add files, etc.), then `git rebase --continue` |
| `squash` | `s` | meld into the previous commit, combining both messages |
| `fixup` | `f` | meld into the previous commit, discarding this commit's message |
| `drop` | `d` | delete the commit (or just delete the line) |
| `exec` | `x` | run a shell command after that commit |
| `break` | `b` | pause the rebase there |

You can also **reorder lines** to reorder commits, and **delete a line** to drop that commit.

**What happens after you save and close the editor:** Git does *not* "automatically re-run" anything on its own. It replays the commits one by one onto `HEAD~3` in the order and with the commands you specified. If you used `squash`/`fixup`, it stops to let you edit the combined message. If you used `edit`, it stops and hands control back to you. If a replay conflicts, it stops and you resolve it, then `git rebase --continue` (or `--abort` to bail out entirely).

**Plain terms:** it's the history-cleanup tool — squash three messy commits into one, reword messages, reorder, or drop a bad commit before pushing. Note it rewrites commit hashes, so don't do it on commits already pushed to a shared branch unless you're prepared to force-push.

One correction to the earlier attempt: the commit types are `pick`/`reword`/`edit`/`squash`/`fixup` (plus `drop`, `exec`, `break`), not "squash/rewrite/edit" — and Git doesn't re-run commits automatically; it replays them and pauses whenever your instructions require input.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  | `edit` | `e` | stop the rebase at that commit so you can amend it (`git commit --amend`, add files, etc.), then `git rebase --continue` |
  | `squash` | `s` | meld into the previous commit, combining both messages |

  | `drop` | `d` | delete the commit (or just delete the line) |
  | `exec` | `x` | run a shell command after that commit |
  | `break` | `b` | pause the rebase there |
  
  You can also **reorder lines** to reorder commits, and **delete a line** to drop that commit.
  
  **What happens after you save and close the editor:** Git does *not* "automatically re-run" anything on its own. It replays the commits one by one onto `HEAD~3` in the order and with the commands you specified. If you used `squash`/`fixup`, it stops to let you edit the combined message. If you used `edit`, it stops and hands control back to you. If a replay conflicts, it stops and you resolve it, then `git rebase --continue` (or `--abort` to bail out entirely).
  
  **Plain terms:** it's the history-cleanup tool — squash three messy commits into one, reword messages, reorder, or drop a bad commit before pushing. Note it rewrites commit hashes, so don't do it on commits already pushed to a shared branch unless you're prepared to force-push.

A **201 Created** status code means a resource was successfully created on the server.

- **HTTP 200 OK** — The request was received and understood (but doesn't necessarily mean creation).
- **HTTP 201 Created** — The server has processed the request and *created* a new resource, typically returned when you POST/PUT/DELETE to create something. This is the standard code for "resource created successfully."

For example, after creating an item via `POST /items`, you'd expect a response like:

```
HTTP/1.1 201 Created
Location: /items/42
Content-Type: application/json
```

Let me know if you need help finding or implementing this in your codebase!

**The simplest failure mode: false consensus — the council reports agreement as confidence, when agreement was guaranteed by construction.**

Here's the mechanism, and it's visible in the code:

**1. All seats share one model.** `resolvePanelModel()` (council.ts:227) resolves a *single* `panelModel`, and the loop at line 234–236 creates every seat with `createProvider({ model: panelModel })`. There is no per-seat model, no provider rotation, no temperature spread. So "N bots from different providers" is not what this design actually does — it's N copies of one brain.

**2. The seats are independent, but not diverse.** The comment at line 232–233 says independence is what makes agreement "genuine." That's the flaw: independence removes *correlated noise*, not *correlated bias*. Five cheap models with the same training-data gap will all miss the same fact, and because they never see each other, they can't correct each other either. Independence guarantees they won't copy each other's mistakes — it does nothing about mistakes they'd make anyway.

**3. The synthesis step then launders that into confidence.** `buildSynthesisPrompt()` (line 69–81) instructs the synthesizer to put "claims most or all agents independently arrived at" under **"## Convergent findings (high confidence)"**. So the output literally promotes unanimous agreement to a confidence label. With a homogeneous panel, unanimity is the *expected* outcome regardless of truth — the confidence signal is measuring model homogeneity, not correctness.

**4. The failure is silent and self-reinforcing.** A shared blind spot produces no disagreement, so it never lands in "## Contested" and never in "## Minority signal." The one section designed to catch a lone dissenter stays empty precisely because there is no dissenter. The user gets a clean, confident, well-formatted report that is wrong in exactly the place they'd most trust it.

**5. Cheap models make it worse in a second way.** The code already documents this at lines 246–272: cheap/tool-happy models hit the 6-turn cap and return nothing, so the fallback either salvages a partial message or asks the model to write findings from its own history. A cheap panel therefore tends to produce *shorter, more generic* findings — which are also more likely to converge, because generic claims are the ones every model agrees on. Homogeneity and shallowness compound.

**The one-line answer:** with N cheap clones, the council's disagreement detector has nothing to detect, so it outputs unanimous-but-wrong findings under a "high confidence" heading — and the more seats you add, the more confident it looks without getting any more correct.

**Cheapest fix, if you want one:** make the panel heterogeneous rather than larger — different model families per seat (that's the whole point of "different providers"), and have the synthesizer treat unanimity as *uninformative* unless at least two distinct model families are represented. Diversity of *source* is the only thing that makes agreement evidence.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

In `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts`, line 111, inside `interface Episode`:

```ts
/** Token usage split by model tier. */
tokensUsed: { archimedes?: number; largeModel?: number };
```

Details:
- **Required field** (no `?` on `tokensUsed` itself) — every `Episode` must supply it.
- **Both inner properties are optional** (`archimedes?`, `largeModel?`) and both are `number` — token counts per model tier.
- It's an **inline anonymous object type**, not a named interface, so it can't be imported or reused elsewhere by name.
- Semantics: `archimedes` = tokens spent by the small model, `largeModel` = tokens spent by the escalation model. Since both are optional, `{}` is valid — e.g. when Archimedes was skipped and no large model ran.

Note the distinct shapes elsewhere, in case you're cross-referencing: `orchestration/specialists.ts:40` uses a flat `tokensUsed: number`, and `orchestration/types.ts:35` uses `tokensUsed?: number`. Only the Archimedes `Episode` uses the split-by-tier object.

```ts
function inferTaskCategory(task: string): TaskCategory {
  const t = task.toLowerCase();
  if (/\b(review|audit|lint|check)\b/.test(t)) return 'review';
  if (/\b(research|explore|find|investigate|understand)\b/.test(t)) return 'research';
  if (/\b(refactor|restructure|rename|migrate)\b/.test(t)) return 'refactor';
  if (/\b(implement|fix|add|write|create|build|update)\b/.test(t)) return 'implementation';
  return 'other';   // line 133 — fallthrough default
}
```

The four regex checks are tried in order against the lower-cased task string; if none match, execution falls through to line 133, which returns the string literal `'other'`.

## Answer

**What it represents:** `durationMs` is the **wall-clock duration of one complete alternation episode, in milliseconds** — the total elapsed real time from the moment `Alternator.run(task)` starts until the final result is settled. It is documented as such at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:112-113`:

```ts
/** Wall-clock duration of the episode in milliseconds. */
durationMs: number;
```

**How it's measured in `alternator.ts`:** a single `Date.now()` delta spanning the whole `run()` body.

- `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:288` — `const startMs = Date.now();` is the **first statement** in `run()`, before the competence assessment, the epsilon probe, the Archimedes attempt, and any escalation.
- `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:489` — `durationMs: Date.now() - startMs,` is set when the `Episode` object is built, after both the Archimedes branch and the large-model branch have finished.

So the timer covers, cumulatively: episode-history load + `assessCompetence` → gate/override/epsilon-probe decisions → `isLocalAvailable` reachability check → the Archimedes `runAgentLoop` (up to 15 turns) → `verifyArchimedesAnswer` → and, if Archimedes failed or was bypassed, the full large-model `runAgentLoop`. It is **not** per-model or per-turn latency — it's the end-to-end cost of the routing decision plus every model call it triggered.

Two details worth knowing:

1. **It excludes persistence.** The episode is saved at line 494, *after* `durationMs` is computed at 489, so disk I/O for `saveEpisode` and the `shouldFineTune` check (lines 493-508) are not counted.
2. **It's a wall clock, not CPU time.** It includes all network round-trips to Ollama/LM Studio and the large-model provider, plus any time the user spends at a `confirmFn` permission prompt.

**Consumers:** `agent/confess.ts:75` and `dream/dream.ts:75` both render it as whole seconds (`Math.round(ep.durationMs / 1000)`), so it feeds the human-readable episode summaries and the dream/lessons digest.

**One correction to the prior attempt's framing:** the `Episode` interface in `dream/episode.ts:11-19` is a *different, smaller* interface (it has `model`, `success`, `tokens` and no `projectRoot`/`archimedes*` fields). `alternator.ts` does not use it — line 16 imports `episodeStore` from `./episode-capture.js`, which imports `Episode` from `./types.js` (`episode-capture.ts:3`). The `durationMs` at `alternator.ts:489` belongs to `archimedes/types.ts:89`.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

**`'normal'`**

In `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:280`, the constructor does:

```ts
this.permissions = opts.permissions ?? new PermissionSystem('normal');
```

So if `permissions` is omitted from `AlternatorOptions`, a fresh `PermissionSystem` is created at the `'normal'` level — deliberately *not* `'auto'`, so the Archimedes attempt can never auto-approve destructive operations the user's own mode would have prompted for (documented in the interface comment at lines 31–36).

**Answer**

The `display` option in `AlternatorOptions` implements the **`Display` interface** from `src/cli/display.ts` (imported as a type at `alternator.ts:7`).

`Display` is the CLI's rendering abstraction — "used by the loop, easy to swap (web UI later)" (`display.ts:6`). It requires these methods: `agentThinking`, `streamText`, `streamEnd`, `toolStart`, `toolCall`, `toolResult`, `toolBlocked`, `warning`, `success`, `error`, `header`, `summary`, `showPlan`, `stepStarted`, `stepCompleted`, plus optional hooks `retry`, `failover`, `circuit`, `contextBar`, `contextDashboard`, `compactionEvent`, `stopThinking`.

**If the option is omitted:** the constructor falls back to a silent no-op implementation — `this.display = opts.display ?? createNoopDisplay()` (`alternator.ts:279`). `createNoopDisplay()` (`alternator.ts:100-121`) returns a `Display` whose methods are all empty functions, so routing and loop events are simply not surfaced to the user; nothing throws and the alternator runs normally. The option is declared optional with the comment "When set, routing and loop events are surfaced to the user" (`alternator.ts:29-30`).

Note: `orchestration/strategy.ts:162` has its own separate `createNoopDisplay()` for the same pattern — the two are independent copies, not shared.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ✓ Done
  4 turns · 5 tool calls

  **Answer**
  
  The `display` option in `AlternatorOptions` implements the **`Display` interface** from `src/cli/display.ts` (imported as a type at `alternator.ts:7`).
  
  `Display` is the CLI's rendering abstraction — "used by the loop, easy to swap (web UI later)" (`display.ts:6`). It requires these methods: `agentThinking`, `streamText`, `streamEnd`, `toolStart`, `toolCall`, `toolResult`, `toolBlocked`, `warning`, `success`, `error`, `header`, `summary`, `showPlan`, `stepStarted`, `stepCompleted`, plus optional hooks `retry`, `failover`, `circuit`, `contextBar`, `contextDashboard`, `compactionEvent`, `stopThinking`.
  
  **If the option is omitted:** the constructor falls back to a silent no-op implementation — `this.display = opts.display ?? createNoopDisplay()` (`alternator.ts:279`). `createNoopDisplay()` (`alternator.ts:100-121`) returns a `Display` whose methods are all empty functions, so routing and loop events are simply not surfaced to the user; nothing throws and the alternator runs normally. The option is declared optional with the comment "When set, routing and loop events are surfaced to the user" (`alternator.ts:29-30`).
  
  Note: `orchestration/strategy.ts:162` has its own separate `createNoopDisplay()` for the same pattern — the two are independent copies, not shared.

Found it. In `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts`, the `tokenize` helper is defined at **lines 50–57**:

```typescript
function tokenize(text: string): string[] {
  if (typeof text !== 'string') return [];
  return text
    .toLowerCase()
    .replace(/[^a-z0-9\s]/g, ' ')
    .split(/\s+/)
    .filter(t => t.length > 2);
}
```

The **character-length filtering rule** is on line 56:

```typescript
.filter(t => t.length > 2)
```

This means:
1. The input text is lowercased and non-alphanumeric characters (except whitespace) are replaced with spaces.
2. It is split into tokens by whitespace (`/\s+/`).
3. **Only tokens whose length is strictly greater than 2** survive the filter — i.e., tokens of length ≤ 2 (single letters, two-character words like "is", "the", etc.) are removed.

So any token with `t.length <= 2` is dropped before returning the array.

## `tasksSimilar` — `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:71-78`

It's a private helper that answers one question: *"is this past episode's task the same kind of work as the task I'm about to route?"* It's used in exactly one place — `assessCompetence` at line 161, where it filters the episode history down to the ones relevant to the current task:

```ts
const matched = list.filter(e => tasksSimilar(safeTask, e.task));
```

That `matched` set then drives the whole routing decision (attempt count, success rate, pattern key).

### The check conditions, in order

The function is a short-circuit ladder — it returns at the first condition that fires, so the expensive token comparison only runs if everything before it fails:

1. **Normalize** (lines 72-73): both strings are `trim()`ed and lowercased into `na` / `nb`.
2. **Empty guard** (line 74): if either normalized string is empty → `false`. This is the only "reject" branch; everything else is a path to `true`.
3. **Exact match** (line 75): `na === nb` → `true`. Cheapest possible hit.
4. **Substring containment** (line 76): `na.includes(nb) || nb.includes(na)` → `true`. So `"fix the login bug"` matches `"fix login bug in auth"` without any tokenization. Note this is a raw character-level check on the *whole* string, not on tokens.
5. **Token similarity** (line 77): only now does it call `similarity(na, nb)` and compare against `SIMILARITY_THRESHOLD` (line 23) = **0.35**.

### What the token similarity actually computes

`similarity` (lines 59-69) is **Jaccard index over token sets**:

- `tokenize` (lines 50-57) lowercases, replaces every non-`[a-z0-9\s]` char with a space, splits on whitespace, and **drops tokens of length ≤ 2** — so `"a"`, `"to"`, `"is"`, `"of"` vanish, and so does any punctuation-only fragment.
- Sets are used, so repeated words count once (word frequency is ignored).
- If either set is empty → `0` (line 62). This is a second, token-level empty guard: a task like `"do it"` tokenizes to `[]` and can never reach the threshold.
- Score = `|intersection| / |union|`, with a `union === 0` guard returning `0`.

So `"refactor the parser module"` vs `"refactor parser tests"` → tokens `{refactor, the, parser, module}` vs `{refactor, parser, tests}` → intersection 2, union 5 → `0.4` → similar.

### Two things worth flagging

- **The containment check is asymmetric in effect.** Because step 4 fires on any substring relation, a very short task string that happens to be a substring of a long one is treated as identical — e.g. `"test"` matches `"run the integration test suite"` outright, bypassing the 0.35 threshold entirely. That's a deliberate recall-over-precision tradeoff (better to over-match history than to under-match), but it means the threshold is not the real gate for short tasks.
- **Cost is O(n·m) over the episode list.** `assessCompetence` runs `tasksSimilar` against every episode, and each call tokenizes both strings. Tokenization is repeated for the current task on every iteration — a trivial memoization win if the episode list ever gets large, but not worth doing now: episode lists here are bounded by `MAX_EXAMPLES`-scale history and this is not a hot path.

No code was changed — this was a read-only explanation.

Found it. Here's what happens in `tasksSimilar`:

```javascript
function tasksSimilar(task: string, other: string): boolean {
  const na = task.trim().toLowerCase();
  const nb = other.trim().toLowerCase();
  if (!na || !nb) return false;
  if (na === nb) return true;
  if (na.includes(nb) || nb.includes(na)) return true;   // <-- line 76
  ...
}
```

For `task = 'run test'` and `other = 'run'`:
- `na` → `'run test'`
- `nb` → `'run'`

The third condition checks:
```javascript
if (na.includes(nb) || nb.includes(na)) return true;
```
Since **`'run test'.includes('run')`** is `true` — the word "run" appears as a substring at the start of "run test" — it returns `true` immediately, **before even reaching the similarity threshold check**.

In short: `'run'` is contained within `'run test'`, so one string includes the other as a substring and they are deemed similar. This is by design (line 76), but it can cause false positives for tasks that share common words or prefixes without being semantically related.

**Why this happens:** The function uses simple `includes()` substring matching, not word-level tokenization. So `'run'` matches inside any string containing "run" as a substring — e.g., 'run test', 'running', 'unrun' (no), etc.

If you want to avoid these false positives, the more robust path is line 77: `return similarity(na, nb) >= SIMILARITY_THRESHOLD;` which uses the token-based `similarity()` function with a threshold of `0.35`. But for this specific case ('run test' vs 'run'), it short-circuits at line 76 and never gets to that step.

**Answer: It returns true immediately because `'run test'.includes('run')` is `true`, triggering the substring check on line 76 before the similarity threshold comparison.**

**Answer: `0`.**

In this codebase the Jaccard similarity lives in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:59-69`, and it short-circuits before doing any set math:

```ts
function similarity(a: string, b: string): number {
  const ta = new Set(tokenize(a));
  const tb = new Set(tokenize(b));
  if (ta.size === 0 || tb.size === 0) return 0;   // ← line 62
  ...
  const union = ta.size + tb.size - intersection;
  return union === 0 ? 0 : intersection / union;  // ← line 68, belt-and-braces
}
```

So:
- **One side empty** → `0`. Mathematically this is the correct limit: intersection is 0, union is non-zero, so 0/|union| = 0.
- **Both sides empty** → `0`, not `NaN`. This is the case that needs an explicit guard: 0/0 is undefined, and in JS `0/0 === NaN`. Line 62 catches it, and line 68 is a redundant second guard for the same condition.

Why `0` and not `1`: returning `1` would mean "two empty token sets are identical", which would make every un-tokenizable task (empty string, punctuation-only, or all tokens ≤2 chars — `tokenize` filters `t.length > 2` at line 56) match every other one. That would poison the episode-matching at line 161 (`list.filter(e => tasksSimilar(safeTask, e.task))`) and let Archimedes claim competence on a pattern it has never actually seen. `0` is the fail-safe choice: no evidence of similarity → no match → falls through to the fallback model.

Two related behaviours worth knowing, since they're the practical consequence:
- `tasksSimilar` (line 71) returns `false` for empty/whitespace-only strings *before* ever calling `similarity` (line 74), so the empty case is doubly guarded.
- `taskPatternFrom` (line 83) maps an empty token list to the slug `'generic'`, so empty tasks still get a stable, shared pattern key rather than an empty one.

No code change was needed — the guard is already correct and complete.

  ```ts
  function similarity(a: string, b: string): number {

    ...

    return union === 0 ? 0 : intersection / union;  // ← line 68, belt-and-braces
  }
  ```
  
  So:
  - **One side empty** → `0`. Mathematically this is the correct limit: intersection is 0, union is non-zero, so 0/|union| = 0.
  - **Both sides empty** → `0`, not `NaN`. This is the case that needs an explicit guard: 0/0 is undefined, and in JS `0/0 === NaN`. Line 62 catches it, and line 68 is a redundant second guard for the same condition.
  
  Why `0` and not `1`: returning `1` would mean "two empty token sets are identical", which would make every un-tokenizable task (empty string, punctuation-only, or all tokens ≤2 chars — `tokenize` filters `t.length > 2` at line 56) match every other one. That would poison the episode-matching at line 161 (`list.filter(e => tasksSimilar(safeTask, e.task))`) and let Archimedes claim competence on a pattern it has never actually seen. `0` is the fail-safe choice: no evidence of similarity → no match → falls through to the fallback model.

  - `tasksSimilar` (line 71) returns `false` for empty/whitespace-only strings *before* ever calling `similarity` (line 74), so the empty case is doubly guarded.

# `competenceThreshold` and `minAttempts` in `ArchimedesConfig`

Both live in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:39-65`, with defaults at `types.ts:68-76`:

| Param | Default | Sanitized as |
|---|---|---|
| `competenceThreshold` | `0.7` | `clamp01(...)` — forced into `[0,1]` (`competence.ts:39-41`) |
| `minAttempts` | `3` | `Math.max(0, ...)` — non-negative integer-ish (`competence.ts:42`) |

## What each one means

**`competenceThreshold`** — the minimum historical success rate Archimedes must have on a *similar* task before it is trusted to run without escalation. It's compared against `successRate = successes / attempts` computed from past episodes (`competence.ts:101-104`).

**`minAttempts`** — a warm-up floor. Until a pattern has at least this many recorded Archimedes attempts, the threshold is **not consulted at all**. The doc comment is explicit: *"Below this count, Archimedes always gets a chance to gather training data"* (`types.ts:51-55`).

## How they interact during routing

The routing decision is `assessCompetence()` in `competence.ts:141-202`, called from the alternator at `alternator.ts:314`. The two parameters form a strict **two-stage gate, evaluated in this order**:

1. **`enabled` check** (`competence.ts:150`) — if false, escalate immediately; neither parameter is read.
2. **Learning phase — `minAttempts` wins** (`competence.ts:168-176`):
   `if (level.attemptCount < cfg.minAttempts)` → `useArchimedes: true`, unconditionally. A brand-new pattern with 0 attempts and a 0% success rate still goes to Archimedes. Confidence ramps `0.3 → 0.6` as attempts approach the minimum.
3. **Competence gate — `competenceThreshold` applies** (`competence.ts:178-194`), only once `attemptCount >= minAttempts`:
   - `successRate >= competenceThreshold` → `useArchimedes: true`
   - otherwise → `useArchimedes: false`, escalate to `DEFAULT_FALLBACK_MODEL` (`claude-sonnet-4-5-20251001`, `competence.ts:14`)

So `minAttempts` is a **precondition that short-circuits** `competenceThreshold`. Raising `minAttempts` makes the system more permissive (more small-model traffic, more training data); raising `competenceThreshold` makes it stricter (more escalation) — but only for patterns that have already cleared the attempt floor.

## Two details that matter in practice

- **The attempt count is per-pattern, not global.** Patterns come from `taskPatternFrom()` (`competence.ts:83-88`): `category:first_8_tokens`. Episodes are matched by fuzzy similarity (`tasksSimilar`, Jaccard ≥ `0.35`, `competence.ts:71-78`), so a *new* task phrasing starts back at 0 attempts and re-enters the learning phase. `minAttempts` is therefore paid repeatedly across distinct task shapes.
- **The gate can't freeze.** Once a pattern is gated, it would never record another attempt — so `epsilonProbeRate` (default `0.05`) overrides the gate with that probability (`alternator.ts:331-343`). Note the probe only fires when the gate actually fired; a pattern in its `minAttempts` phase is already using Archimedes and needs no probe. A manual `:small1` override (`alternator.ts:320-329`) bypasses the gate deterministically, applied *before* the dice roll.

One caveat worth flagging: `assessCompetence` derives `attemptCount` from the *matched episode window* (`RECENT_EPISODE_LIMIT` at `alternator.ts:313`), not from the persisted `CompetenceLevel.attemptCount` that `updateCompetence` maintains (`competence.ts:229`). With a small recent-episode window, a pattern with a long history can look like it's still in its learning phase and keep getting Archimedes — the `minAttempts` floor is effectively re-applied per window rather than once per pattern lifetime.

  | `minAttempts` | `3` | `Math.max(0, ...)` — non-negative integer-ish (`competence.ts:42`) |
  
  ## What each one means
  
  **`competenceThreshold`** — the minimum historical success rate Archimedes must have on a *similar* task before it is trusted to run without escalation. It's compared against `successRate = successes / attempts` computed from past episodes (`competence.ts:101-104`).
  
  **`minAttempts`** — a warm-up floor. Until a pattern has at least this many recorded Archimedes attempts, the threshold is **not consulted at all**. The doc comment is explicit: *"Below this count, Archimedes always gets a chance to gather training data"* (`types.ts:51-55`).
  
  ## How they interact during routing
  
  The routing decision is `assessCompetence()` in `competence.ts:141-202`, called from the alternator at `alternator.ts:314`. The two parameters form a strict **two-stage gate, evaluated in this order**:
  
  1. **`enabled` check** (`competence.ts:150`) — if false, escalate immediately; neither parameter is read.
  2. **Learning phase — `minAttempts` wins** (`competence.ts:168-176`):
     `if (level.attemptCount < cfg.minAttempts)` → `useArchimedes: true`, unconditionally. A brand-new pattern with 0 attempts and a 0% success rate still goes to Archimedes. Confidence ramps `0.3 → 0.6` as attempts approach the minimum.
  3. **Competence gate — `competenceThreshold` applies** (`competence.ts:178-194`), only once `attemptCount >= minAttempts`:
     - `successRate >= competenceThreshold` → `useArchimedes: true`
     - otherwise → `useArchimedes: false`, escalate to `DEFAULT_FALLBACK_MODEL` (`claude-sonnet-4-5-20251001`, `competence.ts:14`)

Raising `minAttempts` makes the system more permissive (more small-model traffic, more training data); raising `competenceThreshold` makes it stricter (more escalation) — but only for patterns that have already cleared the attempt floor.
  
  ## Two details that matter in practice
  
  - **The attempt count is per-pattern, not global.** Patterns come from `taskPatternFrom()` (`competence.ts:83-88`): `category:first_8_tokens`. Episodes are matched by fuzzy similarity (`tasksSimilar`, Jaccard ≥ `0.35`, `competence.ts:71-78`), so a *new* task phrasing starts back at 0 attempts and re-enters the learning phase. `minAttempts` is therefore paid repeatedly across distinct task shapes.
  - **The gate can't freeze.** Once a pattern is gated, it would never record another attempt — so `epsilonProbeRate` (default `0.05`) overrides the gate with that probability (`alternator.ts:331-343`). Note the probe only fires when the gate actually fired; a pattern in its `minAttempts` phase is already using Archimedes and needs no probe. A manual `:small1` override (`alternator.ts:320-329`) bypasses the gate deterministically, applied *before* the dice roll.
  
  One caveat worth flagging: `assessCompetence` derives `attemptCount` from the *matched episode window* (`RECENT_EPISODE_LIMIT` at `alternator.ts:313`), not from the persisted `CompetenceLevel.attemptCount` that `updateCompetence` maintains (`competence.ts:229`). **File:** `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts`, lines 85–116.

**What `Episode` records about models used:**

- `archimedesAttempted: boolean` (line 99) — whether the small model (Archimedes) was invoked at all.
- `archimedesSucceeded: boolean` (line 101) — whether Archimedes's output was accepted without large-model intervention.
- `archimedesOutput?: string` (line 103) — raw text Archimedes produced, if attempted.
- `largeModelUsed?: string` (line 105) — the **large-model id** used when Archimedes failed or was bypassed (e.g. `claude-sonnet-4-5`).
- `largeModelOutput?: string` (line 107) — final output from the large model, if any.
- `tokensUsed: { archimedes?: number; largeModel?: number }` (line 111) — token usage split by model tier.

**When `largeModelUsed` is a string vs `undefined`:**

It is set at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:482`:

```ts
largeModelUsed: usedArchimedes ? undefined : largeModelProvider.model,
```

- **`undefined`** — when `usedArchimedes` is true, i.e. the task was routed to Archimedes and the large model was never invoked.
- **a string** — when `usedArchimedes` is false, i.e. the task was escalated to (or bypassed Archimedes for) the large model; the value is that provider's model id.

Note the field is optional (`?`), so `undefined` is the "no large model touched this episode" signal — the same condition that also blanks `largeModelOutput` and `tokensUsed.largeModel` (lines 483, 487). Consumers rely on this: `episode-capture.ts:119` counts `ep.largeModelUsed` as a large-model intervention, and `dream/dream.ts:72` reads it the same way.

  - `largeModelUsed?: string` (line 105) — the **large-model id** used when Archimedes failed or was bypassed (e.g. `claude-sonnet-4-5`).
  - `largeModelOutput?: string` (line 107) — final output from the large model, if any.
  - `tokensUsed: { archimedes?: number; largeModel?: number }` (line 111) — token usage split by model tier.
  
  **When `largeModelUsed` is a string vs `undefined`:**
  
  It is set at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:482`:
  
  ```ts
  largeModelUsed: usedArchimedes ? undefined : largeModelProvider.model,
  ```
  
  - **`undefined`** — when `usedArchimedes` is true, i.e. the task was routed to Archimedes and the large model was never invoked.
  - **a string** — when `usedArchimedes` is false, i.e. the task was escalated to (or bypassed Archimedes for) the large model; the value is that provider's model id.
  
  Note the field is optional (`?`), so `undefined` is the "no large model touched this episode" signal — the same condition that also blanks `largeModelOutput` and `tokensUsed.largeModel` (lines 483, 487). Consumers rely on this: `episode-capture.ts:119` counts `ep.largeModelUsed` as a large-model intervention, and `dream/dream.ts:72` reads it the same way.

**`shouldFineTune`** — `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:299-313`

It's a pure predicate that answers: *"have enough Archimedes failures piled up to justify a fine-tune pass?"*

How it works:
1. **Threshold resolution** (line 304): `Math.max(1, minFailures ?? DEFAULT_MIN_FAILURES)` — clamps the caller-supplied value to at least 1, and falls back to the default if `null`/`undefined` is passed.
2. **Input sanitising** (line 305): runs the list through `safeEpisodes(...)`, which filters out malformed entries so a bad episode can't blow up the count.
3. **Failure count** (lines 306-308): counts episodes where `archimedesAttempted && !archimedesSucceeded` — i.e. Archimedes was tried but the large model had to intervene or the reviewer rejected the output. Note this is a plain `filter().length`, O(n) over the episode list, no nesting.
4. **Decision** (line 309): returns `failures >= threshold`.
5. **Never throws** (lines 310-312): the whole body is wrapped in `try/catch` returning `false`, so invalid input degrades to "don't fine-tune" rather than crashing the caller.

**Default threshold: 20 failures** — `DEFAULT_MIN_FAILURES = 20`, declared at `competence.ts:17` and used as the default parameter value on line 301.

Callers: `alternator.ts:15` and `episode-capture.ts:4` (both import it), and it's re-exported from `index.ts:8`.

**File:** `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts`

## How similarity is calculated

Two layers, both in the internal helpers section:

**1. `similarity(a, b)` — lines 59–69: Jaccard index over token sets**

- `tokenize()` (lines 50–57) lowercases the string, replaces every non-alphanumeric character with a space, splits on whitespace, and **drops tokens of length ≤ 2** (so "a", "to", "is" don't count).
- Each string becomes a `Set` of tokens (duplicates collapse).
- If either set is empty → returns `0`.
- Otherwise it counts the intersection and computes `intersection / union`, where `union = |A| + |B| − intersection`. That is the classic Jaccard similarity, range 0–1.

**2. `tasksSimilar(task, other)` — lines 71–78: the actual gate**

It short-circuits before ever computing Jaccard:
1. Trim + lowercase both; if either is empty → `false`.
2. Exact match → `true`.
3. Substring containment either way (`na.includes(nb) || nb.includes(na)`) → `true`.
4. Otherwise → `similarity(na, nb) >= SIMILARITY_THRESHOLD`.

## Default threshold

`SIMILARITY_THRESHOLD = 0.35` — line 23, a module-level `const` (not exported, not configurable via `ArchimedesConfig`). So two tasks count as the same pattern when at least 35% of their combined distinct token vocabulary overlaps.

## Where it's used

`assessCompetence()` line 161: `list.filter(e => tasksSimilar(safeTask, e.task))` — this is the only call site, and it's what decides which historical episodes feed `buildLevelFromEpisodes()` and therefore the success-rate routing decision.

One thing worth flagging: `tasksSimilar` is O(n·m) per episode pair (set build + intersection scan), and `assessCompetence` runs it against every episode in the list — so the whole assessment is O(E·T) in episodes × tokens. Fine at current scale, but it's a linear scan with no indexing if the episode log grows large.

  ## How similarity is calculated
  
  Two layers, both in the internal helpers section:
  
  **1. `similarity(a, b)` — lines 59–69: Jaccard index over token sets**
  
  - `tokenize()` (lines 50–57) lowercases the string, replaces every non-alphanumeric character with a space, splits on whitespace, and **drops tokens of length ≤ 2** (so "a", "to", "is" don't count).
  - Each string becomes a `Set` of tokens (duplicates collapse).
  - If either set is empty → returns `0`.
  - Otherwise it counts the intersection and computes `intersection / union`, where `union = |A| + |B| − intersection`. That is the classic Jaccard similarity, range 0–1.
  
  **2. `tasksSimilar(task, other)` — lines 71–78: the actual gate**
  
  It short-circuits before ever computing Jaccard:
  1. Trim + lowercase both; if either is empty → `false`.
  2. Exact match → `true`.
  3. Substring containment either way (`na.includes(nb) || nb.includes(na)`) → `true`.
  4. Otherwise → `similarity(na, nb) >= SIMILARITY_THRESHOLD`.
  
  ## Default threshold
  
  `SIMILARITY_THRESHOLD = 0.35` — line 23, a module-level `const` (not exported, not configurable via `ArchimedesConfig`). So two tasks count as the same pattern when at least 35% of their combined distinct token vocabulary overlaps.
  
  ## Where it's used
  
  `assessCompetence()` line 161: `list.filter(e => tasksSimilar(safeTask, e.task))` — this is the only call site, and it's what decides which historical episodes feed `buildLevelFromEpisodes()` and therefore the success-rate routing decision.
  
  One thing worth flagging: `tasksSimilar` is O(n·m) per episode pair (set build + intersection scan), and `assessCompetence` runs it against every episode in the list — so the whole assessment is O(E·T) in episodes × tokens. Fine at current scale, but it's a linear scan with no indexing if the episode log grows large.
